Let me run a tighter query — aggregated at the pattern level with coverage per channel — to give you clean, usable logic.# Derivation Logic + Coverage for the 3 Missing Event Flags

Based on 3 days of UDI (~836M rows total), here's what each concept looks like in actual production data.

📞 1. event_click_to_call — Phone call initiations
What exists: Very few hits literally use "click to call" / "tap to call" / "call us" phrasing. Click-to-call is not a well-tagged pattern in UDI — most phone-tap behavior is embedded inside support flows and not labeled with a dedicated action.

Coverage (last 3 days)
Channel	Matching rows	Total rows	% Fill
App	18,249	702.8M	0.0026%
Web	2,717	133.4M	0.0020%
Suggested derivation (best approximation)
```
CASE
WHEN attribute_event_action ILIKE '%click to call%'
OR attribute_event_action ILIKE '%click-to-call%'
OR attribute_event_action ILIKE '%tap to call%'
OR attribute_event_action ILIKE '%call us%'
THEN 1 ELSE 0
END AS event_click_to_call
```

⚠️ Realistic expectation: This flag will fire on ~1 in 50,000 rows. If "click to call" matters to your consumers, flag this as a tagging gap — don't present the derived column as reliable.

💬 2. event_chat_engage — Chat interactions
What exists: Chat is very well tagged — both via attribute_event_category (whole category) and attribute_event_action (specific chat moments). Key categories: Chat, JourneyBot, Live Agent. Top actions include Chat Topics Viewed, Chatbot Screen Load, Chat Click, Chat Entry Click, Live Agent Chat Initiation.

Coverage (last 3 days)
Channel	Matching rows	Total rows	% Fill
App	15,089,657	702.8M	2.15%
Web	1,128,208	133.4M	0.85%
Suggested derivation — pick based on intent
```
-- Broad: any chat touchpoint (screen view, button click, bot interaction)
CASE
WHEN attribute_event_category ILIKE '%chat%'
OR attribute_event_category ILIKE '%journeybot%'
OR attribute_event_action ILIKE '%chat%'
THEN 1 ELSE 0
END AS event_chat_engage
```

Narrower alternative — true engagement only (initiated or messaged, not just screen view):
```
CASE
WHEN attribute_event_action IN (
'Chat Click', 'Chat Entry Click', 'Chat Message Engaged',
'Live Agent Chat Initiation', 'Chat Session Ended', 'Chat Ended By User'
)
OR attribute_event_action ILIKE '%chat with customer care%click%'
THEN 1 ELSE 0
END AS event_chat_engage_strict
```

The broad version matches ~2% of app hits; the strict version will land around 0.3–0.5%. Choose based on whether your downstream consumers want "touched chat" or "actually engaged chat."

🏪 3. event_store_search — Store locator searches
What exists: Also lightly tagged. Store locator usage mostly fires as page views (page_url_path containing /stores/ or /store-locator), not as a dedicated event action.

Coverage (last 3 days — using action-string match only)
Channel	Matching rows	Total rows	% Fill
App	252	702.8M	~0%
Web	6,114	133.4M	0.0046%
Suggested derivation — combine action + URL
```
CASE
WHEN attribute_event_action ILIKE '%store locator%'
OR attribute_event_action ILIKE '%find a store%'
OR attribute_event_action ILIKE '%find store%'
OR attribute_event_action ILIKE '%store search%'
OR page_url_path ILIKE '%/stores/%'
OR page_url_path ILIKE '%/store-locator%'
OR page_name ILIKE '%store locator%'
THEN 1 ELSE 0
END AS event_store_search
```

Adding the URL/page_name signals will boost web coverage significantly (likely to 0.1–0.3% of web rows). App store-locator is nearly nonexistent — likely because the T-Life app has very different store discovery UX.

📝 Recommendations for your MIP projection
Flag	Verdict	Recommendation
event_click_to_call	🔴 Not reliably tagged (~0.002%)	Include the derivation but flag as low-fidelity in your MIP contract notes. Consider filing a tagging-gap ticket.
event_chat_engage	🟢 Well tagged (~1–2%)	Include confidently. Pick broad or strict based on consumer need.
event_store_search	🟡 Partial — mostly URL-based	Use the combined action+URL rule, not action alone. Document it as approximate.




Define peer set and topline metrics.

### Impact on topline

**Plain version:** how much of the topline's movement this one value is responsible for. It answers "so what" rather than "how much did this change."

**Formula, counts:**

```
impact % = (slice current − slice baseline) ÷ topline baseline
```

The denominator is the topline, not the slice's own base. That's the whole trick.

**Example:**

```
Paid Search  (493,775 − 446,463) ÷ 1,720,000  =  +2.75%
SMS          ( 31,094 − 202,108) ÷ 1,720,000  =  −9.94%
```

Compare against what each did on its own base: Paid Search rose 10.6%, SMS fell 84.6%. Sorted by own change, SMS looks like the story of the week and Paid Search looks minor. Sorted by impact, SMS moved the topline by ten points and Paid Search by three. That ordering is the one you want in the weekly read.

## Versus peer set — worked through step by step

**The question it answers:** did this channel move differently from all the other channels?

### The setup

Channel breakout, Total UPV, week 7 compared to week 6.

Week 6

Week 7

**All channels together (topline)**

1,720,000

1,545,000

**Paid Search**

446,463

493,775

The topline fell. Paid Search rose. That gap is what we're measuring.

### Step 1 — What did Paid Search do?

```
493,775 − 446,463 = +47,312
+47,312 ÷ 446,463 = +10.6%
```

**Paid Search went up 10.6%.**

### Step 2 — What did every other channel do?

Take Paid Search out of the topline. What's left is the peer set: organic, direct, email, SMS, display, and the rest, all added together.

```
Week 6 peers:  1,720,000 − 446,463 = 1,273,537
Week 7 peers:  1,545,000 − 493,775 = 1,051,225
```

Now the same change calculation on that combined group:

```
1,051,225 − 1,273,537 = −222,312
−222,312 ÷ 1,273,537 = −17.5%
```

**Every other channel, combined, went down 17.5%.**

### Step 3 — The points gap

```
Paid Search:   +10.6%
Peer set:      −17.5%
Gap:            10.6 − (−17.5) = +28.1 points
```

**Paid Search finished 28.1 points ahead of the rest of the channel mix.**

### Step 4 — Turn that into volume

Points are hard to act on. So: what would Paid Search have looked like if it had behaved like everything else?

```
Apply the peer rate to Paid Search's starting point:
446,463 × (1 − 0.175) = 368,527
```

That's the counterfactual. Now compare it to reality:

```
Actual:            493,775
Would have been:   368,527
Excess:           +125,248
```

**125,248 UPV exists only because Paid Search moved against the tide.**

### How to say it out loud

> Every other channel dropped about 17% this week. Paid Search went up 10%. If it had dropped with everything else it would have come in around 369,000. It came in at 494,000. So roughly 125,000 visits are down to something happening specifically in Paid Search.

### The same four steps on SMS, for contrast

```
1. SMS:        31,094 vs 202,108     →  −84.6%
2. Peer set:   1,513,906 vs 1,517,892 →   −0.3%
3. Gap:        −84.6 − (−0.3)         =  −84.4 points
4. Would have been: 202,108 × 0.997   =  201,577
   Excess:     31,094 − 201,577       =  −170,483
```

The rest of the channels were flat. SMS fell off a cliff. Nothing systemic is going on, so the answer is entirely inside SMS.

### Two things the engineers need to know

**The peer set always uses the four-week trend**, whatever comparison the user has picked on screen. The example above uses prior week because it's easier to follow.

**Step 2 cannot be done by subtraction on real data.** It worked here because the sample file was built so the channels add up. Real UPV doesn't: one person visits through search and direct in the same week and counts in both, so the channels sum to more than the topline. Subtracting Paid Search from 1,720,000 would give the wrong peer figure.

On EDL, step 2 means going back to the visit-level table and counting distinct visitors across all channels except Paid Search. Then repeating that for every channel, every breakout, every metric, every week. Page views is the only metric where the shortcut is valid.



I wanted to clarify one point on the peer-set definition for unique visitor metrics.
For example, say Visitor A comes through both Paid Search and Direct during the same week. When calculating the peer set for Paid Search, should Visitor A:
1. Be excluded from the peer set because they had any Paid Search activity during the week, even though they also used Direct?
or
2. Be assigned to one attributed weekly channel first, and only excluded if Paid Search is that attributed channel?
For example, if we have:
Paid Search visitors: A, B, C  
Direct visitors: A, D, E
For the Paid Search peer set, should we count D and E only, because A touched Paid Search and therefore must be excluded? Or if A's attributed weekly channel is Direct, should A, D, E all be part of the Paid Search peer set?
I want to confirm because the first definition requires us to retain all channel memberships per visitor/week, whereas the second lets us use the single attributed weekly channel we already have.


SELECT
    sourceTable,

    COUNT(*) AS rows,

    COUNT_IF(
        sessionEntryPageUrlFull IS NOT NULL
        AND TRIM(sessionEntryPageUrlFull) <> ''
    ) AS rowsWithEntryUrl,

    COUNT_IF(utmSource <> '(not set)') AS rowsWithUtmSource,
    COUNT_IF(utmMedium <> '(not set)') AS rowsWithUtmMedium,
    COUNT_IF(utmCampaign <> '(not set)') AS rowsWithUtmCampaign,

    COUNT_IF(channelName IS NOT NULL) AS rowsWithChannel,
    COUNT_IF(externalCampaignCode IS NOT NULL) AS rowsWithExternalCampaign

FROM prdrzranalytics.lab42.sdi_tbl_mip_silver_detailsPerHit_daily
WHERE eventDate BETWEEN DATE '2026-09-20' AND DATE '2026-09-29'
GROUP BY sourceTable
ORDER BY sourceTable;
